========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, work made me come in. I just... I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders. I’ll promise people I’ll do a basic follow-up on a repair, and it just vanishes from my head. I do general maintenance, you know, so there's always a lot of moving parts. 

Like the other day, I was working on this HVAC unit—and let me tell you, the new filters the management company buys are just absolute garbage. They disintegrate if you even look at them wrong. I don't even know why they bother saving the two dollars. My brother said the exact same thing about his supplier, he works out in Florida now. I could never do Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. 

But... wait, what was I saying? 

Right, the work orders. Yeah, the forgotten follow-ups have just piled up so much lately. It's gotten really bad. My supervisor Dave—he's a good guy, usually pretty laid back, always brings these amazing apple fritters from that bakery on 5th street on Fridays—he basically told me I had to come see a doctor. He said if I don't get this sorted out, I'm gonna lose my shot at moving up to a lead position. So... yeah, that's why I'm here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... I remember being like this way back in grade school, honestly. Long before I ever had to deal with Dave or work orders or any of that pressure. My teachers always told my parents I was a bright kid, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. Like, I’d do the homework, I swear I did, but by the time I got to school, it was just... gone. Or I'd miss half the directions when the teacher was talking. 

Actually, my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was this older lady who always wore these really loud, chunky necklaces—she had this system where she’d give out little gold star stickers. But not the cheap paper ones, the shiny foil ones. I loved those stickers. I’d try so hard to get one, but she always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I'd just be staring out the window, looking at the playground. They had this huge oak tree out there, and I used to watch the squirrels chasing each other up the trunk. You don't really see trees like that anymore, you know? They cut it down a few years ago to expand the parking lot, which is a shame. 

But yeah, the forgetfulness... it's definitely been there since I was a kid.

They disintegrate if you even look at them wrong. I don't even know why they bother saving the two dollars. My brother said the exact same thing about his supplier, he works out in Florida now. I could never do Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? \n\nRight, the work orders. Yeah, the forgotten follow-ups have just piled up so much lately. So... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or forgetfulness problems as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I remember being like this way back in grade school, honestly. Long before I ever had to deal with Dave or work orders or any of that pressure. My teachers always told my parents I was a bright kid, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. Like, I’d do the homework, I swear I did, but by the time I got to school, it was just... gone. I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. But it's just me at my place, so there's nobody there to keep track of what I'm doing anyway. I had to wash him with Dawn dish soap in the kitchen sink. \n\nThat stuff really does work miracles, by the way. I use it at work sometimes when I get grease on my uniform. The uniform company management uses is just terrible, though. They shrink the shirts every single time. I asked for a medium and it fits like an extra small now. I told the delivery guy, Frank, about it, but he just drives the truck, he doesn't actually do the laundry. Frank's a nice guy, though, his daughter just started college out in Ohio... \n\nWait. What did you ask me? Something about my house?\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore age 12, did these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't really remember... I mean, twelve is a long time ago. At home, it was just me and my mom. She worked the evening shift at the hospital, so I was usually by myself after school. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. I remember being like this way back in grade school, honestly. Long before I ever had to deal with Dave or work orders or any of that pressure. They cut it down a few years ago to expand the parking lot, which is a shame. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetfulness... it's definitely been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. But it's just me at my place, so there's nobody there to keep track of what I'm doing anyway. Well, me and my cat, Barnaby. He’s this big orange tabby. I actually found him behind the dumpster at my old apartment complex. He was just a tiny thing back then, covered in motor oil of all things. I had to wash him with Dawn dish soap in the kitchen sink. \n\nThat stuff really does work miracles, by the way. I use it at work sometimes when I get grease on my uniform. The uniform company management uses is just terrible, though. They shrink the shirts every single time. I asked for a medium and it fits like an extra small now. I told the delivery guy, Frank, about it, but he just drives the truck, he doesn't actually do the laundry. Frank's a nice guy, though, his daughter just started college out in Ohio... \n\nWait. What did you ask me? Something about my house?\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember... I mean, twelve is a long time ago. At home, it was just me and my mom. She worked the evening shift at the hospital, so I was usually by myself after school. She was a phlebotomist. Always had the craziest stories about people passing out when they saw the needle. I never understood that, honestly. Needles don't bother me at all. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. It used to be those good chocolate chip cookies, the soft ones, but now it's just plain pretzels. Pretzels just dry your mouth out... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, I'm not sure if I was forgetting things at home back then. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. My friend Sarah and I used to go to this trivia night at the pub downtown, but they changed the host a few months ago. The old guy, Mike, he was great. They changed the cheese they use on the nachos. It used to be real cheddar, but now it's that fake liquid stuff that comes out of a pump. I can't stand that stuff. It tastes like plastic. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know about social stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? No, not really. I mean, my job keeps me moving all day anyway. I'm always fixing something, climbing a ladder, crawling under a sink. You wouldn't believe the things people flush down toilets in office buildings. Last week, I had to snake this drain on the fourth floor, and I pulled out an entire pair of sunglasses. Like, the big chunky plastic ones. Who drops their sunglasses in the toilet and just flushes? It took me an hour to get the auger unstuck. 

The maintenance closet on that floor is a disaster, too. The guy who had this route before me, Dave, he never organized anything. Just threw all the spare parts into this one giant bin. I spent a whole afternoon just sorting the PVC fittings from the copper ones. Copper is so expensive right now, you know? You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. 

So, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. I don't really feel like I need to move around or anything.

But not the cheap paper ones, the shiny foil ones. I loved those stickers. I’d try so hard to get one, but she always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I'd just be staring out the window, looking at the playground. They had this huge oak tree out there, and I used to watch the squirrels chasing each other up the trunk. You don't really see trees like that anymore, you know? They cut it down a few years ago to expand the parking lot, which is a shame. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetfulness... it's definitely been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. But it's just me at my place, so there's nobody there to keep track of what I'm doing anyway. Well, me and my cat, Barnaby. He’s this big orange tabby. I actually found him behind the dumpster at my old apartment complex. He was just a tiny thing back then, covered in motor oil of all things. I had to wash him with Dawn dish soap in the kitchen sink. \n\nThat stuff really does work miracles, by the way. I use it at work sometimes when I get grease on my uniform. The uniform company management uses is just terrible, though. They shrink the shirts every single time. I asked for a medium and it fits like an extra small now. I told the delivery guy, Frank, about it, but he just drives the truck, he doesn't actually do the laundry. Frank's a nice guy, though, his daughter just started college out in Ohio... \n\nWait. What did you ask me? Something about my house?\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember... I mean, twelve is a long time ago. At home, it was just me and my mom. She worked the evening shift at the hospital, so I was usually by myself after school. She was a phlebotomist. Always had the craziest stories about people passing out when they saw the needle. I never understood that, honestly. Needles don't bother me at all. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. It used to be those good chocolate chip cookies, the soft ones, but now it's just plain pretzels. Pretzels just dry your mouth out... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, I'm not sure if I was forgetting things at home back then. Like I said, I was mostly on my own, just watching TV or whatever until she got back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention or forgetfulness problems in places other than work, like at home or in social situations, now as an adult?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know. Like I said before, I haven't really noticed it at home. It's just me and Barnaby, so... if I forget to do the dishes or leave a cabinet open, there's nobody yelling at me about it. \n\nBut social situations... I guess? I don't really go out much anymore. My friend Sarah and I used to go to this trivia night at the pub downtown, but they changed the host a few months ago. The old guy, Mike, he was great. He'd always throw in a category about 80s movies, which is totally my jam. I can quote almost the entire script of *The Princess Bride*. But the new host, he's just... he talks way too fast, and the microphone is always echoing, and he asks all these obscure sports questions. I don't care about who won the World Series in 1994, you know? The strike year, right? Or was that '95? Anyway, Sarah stopped wanting to go because the food went downhill, too. They changed the cheese they use on the nachos. It used to be real cheddar, but now it's that fake liquid stuff that comes out of a pump. I can't stand that stuff. It tastes like plastic. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know about social stuff. I just kind of keep to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to move around a lot, even when you’re trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, my job keeps me moving all day anyway. I'm always fixing something, climbing a ladder, crawling under a sink. You wouldn't believe the things people flush down toilets in office buildings. Last week, I had to snake this drain on the fourth floor, and I pulled out an entire pair of sunglasses. Like, the big chunky plastic ones. Who drops their sunglasses in the toilet and just flushes? It took me an hour to get the auger unstuck. \n\nThe maintenance closet on that floor is a disaster, too. The guy who had this route before me, Dave, he never organized anything. Just threw all the spare parts into this one giant bin. I spent a whole afternoon just sorting the PVC fittings from the copper ones. Copper is so expensive right now, you know? You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. I don't really feel like I need to move around or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention or forgetfulness problems in your daily life outside of work, such as at home?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me this a minute ago, right? Or something like it. Like I said, I don't really know if I have those problems at home. It's just me and Barnaby—my cat, I don't think I told you his name before. He's a Maine Coon mix, huge, like twenty pounds of just fluff and attitude. He sheds everywhere. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk from Costco. Costco is crazy on the weekends, though. I tried going last Saturday to get more of those rollers and some of that rotisserie chicken they have—you know, the ones that are somehow still like five bucks? But the parking lot was an absolute nightmare. People just driving the wrong way down the aisles, fighting over spots. I saw this lady in a minivan literally lay on her horn for a solid minute because someone was taking too long to load their trunk. It's just not worth the stress, honestly. I think I'm going to start going on Tuesday mornings when they open. \n\nBut yeah, at home... if I forget something, there's no one around to notice. So I couldn't really tell you.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interrupting? No, I don't think so. If anything, I just sit there and let people talk. Some of the tenants in the buildings I work at, they will just talk your ear off if you let them. Like this one lady, Mrs. Gable, over at the Oakwood complex. I was just there to fix a leaky P-trap under her bathroom sink—which, by the way, was leaking because she kept using that harsh chemical drain cleaner. You really shouldn't use that stuff, it just eats right through the pipes if it sits there too long. 

Anyway, she stood in the doorway the entire time I was on the floor working, just going on and on about her niece's wedding in Ohio. Apparently, the caterer forgot the vegetarian meals, and it was this huge drama because the groom's side was mostly vegan. I've never even been to Ohio. My sister went to Cleveland once for a dental hygienist conference, but her flight got delayed like six hours on the tarmac. Airlines are just a complete mess right now. They charge you for every little thing, even just bringing a regular carry-on bag. And the seats keep getting smaller! I don't know how anyone over six feet tall even fits in them anymore. It's ridiculous. 

But yeah, no, I don't really blurt things out or interrupt. I just kind of quietly let them finish their stories.

I just kind of keep to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to move around a lot, even when you’re trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, my job keeps me moving all day anyway. I'm always fixing something, climbing a ladder, crawling under a sink. You wouldn't believe the things people flush down toilets in office buildings. Last week, I had to snake this drain on the fourth floor, and I pulled out an entire pair of sunglasses. Like, the big chunky plastic ones. Who drops their sunglasses in the toilet and just flushes? It took me an hour to get the auger unstuck. \n\nThe maintenance closet on that floor is a disaster, too. The guy who had this route before me, Dave, he never organized anything. Just threw all the spare parts into this one giant bin. I spent a whole afternoon just sorting the PVC fittings from the copper ones. Copper is so expensive right now, you know? You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. I don't really feel like I need to move around or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention or forgetfulness problems in your daily life outside of work, such as at home?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me this a minute ago, right? Or something like it. Like I said, I don't really know if I have those problems at home. It's just me and Barnaby—my cat, I don't think I told you his name before. He's a Maine Coon mix, huge, like twenty pounds of just fluff and attitude. He sheds everywhere. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk from Costco. Costco is crazy on the weekends, though. I tried going last Saturday to get more of those rollers and some of that rotisserie chicken they have—you know, the ones that are somehow still like five bucks? But the parking lot was an absolute nightmare. People just driving the wrong way down the aisles, fighting over spots. I saw this lady in a minivan literally lay on her horn for a solid minute because someone was taking too long to load their trunk. It's just not worth the stress, honestly. I think I'm going to start going on Tuesday mornings when they open. \n\nBut yeah, at home... if I forget something, there's no one around to notice. So I couldn't really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Interrupting? No, I don't think so. If anything, I just sit there and let people talk. Some of the tenants in the buildings I work at, they will just talk your ear off if you let them. Like this one lady, Mrs. Gable, over at the Oakwood complex. I was just there to fix a leaky P-trap under her bathroom sink—which, by the way, was leaking because she kept using that harsh chemical drain cleaner. You really shouldn't use that stuff, it just eats right through the pipes if it sits there too long. \n\nAnyway, she stood in the doorway the entire time I was on the floor working, just going on and on about her niece's wedding in Ohio. Apparently, the caterer forgot the vegetarian meals, and it was this huge drama because the groom's side was mostly vegan. I've never even been to Ohio. My sister went to Cleveland once for a dental hygienist conference, but her flight got delayed like six hours on the tarmac. Airlines are just a complete mess right now. They charge you for every little thing, even just bringing a regular carry-on bag. And the seats keep getting smaller! I don't know how anyone over six feet tall even fits in them anymore. It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, no, I don't really blurt things out or interrupt. I just kind of quietly let them finish their stories.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually, I remember being this way way back in grade school. This was long before I had any job pressure or anything like that. My teachers always told my parents I was bright, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. I'd miss parts of directions, and they always had to remind me to just finish my classwork. 

It reminds me of my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive collection of those little ceramic frogs. They were everywhere—on her desk, on the windowsills, even on top of the chalkboard. I always wondered where she found so many of them. Did people just know she liked frogs and buy them for her, or did she actively hunt for them at flea markets? I went to a flea market a couple of months ago looking for some vintage tools. You can sometimes find really good quality, old-school wrenches that are made of solid steel, not that cheap alloy stuff they sell now that strips the second you put some torque on it. I found a great pipe wrench, but the guy wanted way too much for it. He was trying to tell me it was an antique, but I could clearly see the brand stamp on it, and they didn't even start making that model until the eighties. People will try to pull a fast one on you if they think you don't know your stuff, especially being a woman in this line of work. You really have to stand your ground. 

But anyway, yeah, I've kind of always been like this, even as a kid.

I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. I don't care about who won the World Series in 1994, you know? The strike year, right? Or was that '95? Anyway, Sarah stopped wanting to go because the food went downhill, too. They changed the cheese they use on the nachos. It used to be real cheddar, but now it's that fake liquid stuff that comes out of a pump. I can't stand that stuff. Restless? Fidgety? No, I wouldn't say so. I'm pretty good at just sitting there, honestly. Like when I'm waiting for the parts supplier to pull an order at the warehouse. Sometimes they take forever. I’ll promise people I’ll do a basic follow-up on a repair, and it just vanishes from my head. I do general maintenance, you know, so there's always a lot of moving parts. \n\nLike the other day, I was working on this HVAC unit—and let me tell you, the new filters the management company buys are just absolute garbage. They disintegrate if you even look at them wrong. I don't even know why they bother saving the two dollars. My brother said the exact same thing about his supplier, he works out in Florida now. I could never do Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? \n\nRight, the work orders. Yeah, the forgotten follow-ups have just piled up so much lately. It's gotten really bad. My supervisor Dave—he's a good guy, usually pretty laid back, always brings these amazing apple fritters from that bakery on 5th street on Fridays—he basically told me I had to come see a doctor. He said if I don't get this sorted out, I'm gonna lose my shot at moving up to a lead position. So... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or forgetfulness problems as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I remember being like this way back in grade school, honestly. Long before I ever had to deal with Dave or work orders or any of that pressure. My teachers always told my parents I was a bright kid, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. Like, I’d do the homework, I swear I did, but by the time I got to school, it was just... gone. But not the cheap paper ones, the shiny foil ones. I loved those stickers. I’d try so hard to get one, but she always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I'd just be staring out the window, looking at the playground. They had this huge oak tree out there, and I used to watch the squirrels chasing each other up the trunk. They cut it down a few years ago to expand the parking lot, which is a shame. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetfulness... it's definitely been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I mean, twelve is a long time ago. At home, it was just me and my mom. She worked the evening shift at the hospital, so I was usually by myself after school. She was a phlebotomist. Always had the craziest stories about people passing out when they saw the needle. I never understood that, honestly. Needles don't bother me at all. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. It used to be those good chocolate chip cookies, the soft ones, but now it's just plain pretzels. Pretzels just dry your mouth out... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, I'm not sure if I was forgetting things at home back then. Like I said, I was mostly on my own, just watching TV or whatever until she got back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention or forgetfulness problems in places other than work, like at home or in social situations, now as an adult?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know. Like I said before, I haven't really noticed it at home. It's just me and Barnaby, so... if I forget to do the dishes or leave a cabinet open, there's nobody yelling at me about it. \n\nBut social situations... I guess? I don't really go out much anymore. My friend Sarah and I used to go to this trivia night at the pub downtown, but they changed the host a few months ago. The old guy, Mike, he was great. He'd always throw in a category about 80s movies, which is totally my jam. I can quote almost the entire script of *The Princess Bride*. You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. But the parking lot was an absolute nightmare. People just driving the wrong way down the aisles, fighting over spots. I saw this lady in a minivan literally lay on her horn for a solid minute because someone was taking too long to load their trunk. It's just not worth the stress, honestly. I think I'm going to start going on Tuesday mornings when they open. \n\nBut yeah, at home... if I forget something, there's no one around to notice. So I couldn't really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Interrupting? No, I don't think so. Like this one lady, Mrs. Gable, over at the Oakwood complex. I was just there to fix a leaky P-trap under her bathroom sink—which, by the way, was leaking because she kept using that harsh chemical drain cleaner. You really shouldn't use that stuff, it just eats right through the pipes if it sits there too long. \n\nAnyway, she stood in the doorway the entire time I was on the floor working, just going on and on about her niece's wedding in Ohio. Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's actually becoming a real problem at work. I'm always hunting for my work tablet, or the master keys, my flashlight, or even just the one specific wrench I literally had in my hand a minute ago. It's gotten bad enough that it actually holds up jobs, and I know it makes me look careless to the property managers.

I don't know who picked that color, but it was a terrible choice. They should have gone with a nice slate gray or maybe a brick facade. Brick holds up so much better in this climate anyway. My uncle had a brick house in Michigan, and that thing survived some massive blizzards. Michigan winters are brutal. I remember visiting for Christmas one year and the snow was piled up higher than the mailbox. We had to shovel for two hours just to get the car out of the driveway. I don't miss the snow at all, honestly. 

But yeah, I was at Willow Creek to fix a jammed garbage disposal, and I set my flashlight down... somewhere. I spent twenty minutes looking for it, tearing apart my tool bag, checking the truck. I finally found it inside the refrigerator. I must have opened the fridge to grab my water bottle and just left the flashlight on the shelf next to the mayonnaise. It's so frustrating.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, work made me come in. I just... I keep forgetting scheduled callbacks and work orders. I’ll promise people I’ll do a basic follow-up on a repair, and it just vanishes from my head. I do general maintenance, you know, so there's always a lot of moving parts. \n\nLike the other day, I was working on this HVAC unit—and let me tell you, the new filters the management company buys are just absolute garbage. They disintegrate if you even look at them wrong. I don't even know why they bother saving the two dollars. My brother said the exact same thing about his supplier, he works out in Florida now. I could never do Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? \n\nRight, the work orders. Yeah, the forgotten follow-ups have just piled up so much lately. It's gotten really bad. My supervisor Dave—he's a good guy, usually pretty laid back, always brings these amazing apple fritters from that bakery on 5th street on Fridays—he basically told me I had to come see a doctor. He said if I don't get this sorted out, I'm gonna lose my shot at moving up to a lead position. So... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or forgetfulness problems as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I remember being like this way back in grade school, honestly. Long before I ever had to deal with Dave or work orders or any of that pressure. My teachers always told my parents I was a bright kid, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. Like, I’d do the homework, I swear I did, but by the time I got to school, it was just... gone. Or I'd miss half the directions when the teacher was talking. \n\nActually, my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable—she was this older lady who always wore these really loud, chunky necklaces—she had this system where she’d give out little gold star stickers. But not the cheap paper ones, the shiny foil ones. I loved those stickers. I’d try so hard to get one, but she always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork. I'd just be staring out the window, looking at the playground. They had this huge oak tree out there, and I used to watch the squirrels chasing each other up the trunk. You don't really see trees like that anymore, you know? They cut it down a few years ago to expand the parking lot, which is a shame. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetfulness... it's definitely been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention and forgetfulness problems outside of work, like at home or in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I mean... I'm not really sure. I haven't really noticed it as much at home, to be honest. But it's just me at my place, so there's nobody there to keep track of what I'm doing anyway. Well, me and my cat, Barnaby. He’s this big orange tabby. I actually found him behind the dumpster at my old apartment complex. He was just a tiny thing back then, covered in motor oil of all things. I had to wash him with Dawn dish soap in the kitchen sink. \n\nThat stuff really does work miracles, by the way. I use it at work sometimes when I get grease on my uniform. The uniform company management uses is just terrible, though. They shrink the shirts every single time. I asked for a medium and it fits like an extra small now. I told the delivery guy, Frank, about it, but he just drives the truck, he doesn't actually do the laundry. Frank's a nice guy, though, his daughter just started college out in Ohio... \n\nWait. What did you ask me? Something about my house?\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, like both at school and at home?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember... I mean, twelve is a long time ago. At home, it was just me and my mom. She worked the evening shift at the hospital, so I was usually by myself after school. She was a phlebotomist. Always had the craziest stories about people passing out when they saw the needle. I never understood that, honestly. Needles don't bother me at all. I give blood every couple of months at the Red Cross drive they do at the community center. You get a free t-shirt sometimes, though they've been getting really stingy with the snacks lately. It used to be those good chocolate chip cookies, the soft ones, but now it's just plain pretzels. Pretzels just dry your mouth out... \n\nBut, uh, anyway, I'm not sure if I was forgetting things at home back then. Like I said, I was mostly on my own, just watching TV or whatever until she got back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention or forgetfulness problems in places other than work, like at home or in social situations, now as an adult?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know. Like I said before, I haven't really noticed it at home. It's just me and Barnaby, so... if I forget to do the dishes or leave a cabinet open, there's nobody yelling at me about it. \n\nBut social situations... I guess? I don't really go out much anymore. My friend Sarah and I used to go to this trivia night at the pub downtown, but they changed the host a few months ago. The old guy, Mike, he was great. He'd always throw in a category about 80s movies, which is totally my jam. I can quote almost the entire script of *The Princess Bride*. But the new host, he's just... he talks way too fast, and the microphone is always echoing, and he asks all these obscure sports questions. I don't care about who won the World Series in 1994, you know? The strike year, right? Or was that '95? Anyway, Sarah stopped wanting to go because the food went downhill, too. They changed the cheese they use on the nachos. It used to be real cheddar, but now it's that fake liquid stuff that comes out of a pump. I can't stand that stuff. It tastes like plastic. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know about social stuff. I just kind of keep to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to move around a lot, even when you’re trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, my job keeps me moving all day anyway. I'm always fixing something, climbing a ladder, crawling under a sink. You wouldn't believe the things people flush down toilets in office buildings. Last week, I had to snake this drain on the fourth floor, and I pulled out an entire pair of sunglasses. Like, the big chunky plastic ones. Who drops their sunglasses in the toilet and just flushes? It took me an hour to get the auger unstuck. \n\nThe maintenance closet on that floor is a disaster, too. The guy who had this route before me, Dave, he never organized anything. Just threw all the spare parts into this one giant bin. I spent a whole afternoon just sorting the PVC fittings from the copper ones. Copper is so expensive right now, you know? You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. I don't really feel like I need to move around or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention or forgetfulness problems in your daily life outside of work, such as at home?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me this a minute ago, right? Or something like it. Like I said, I don't really know if I have those problems at home. It's just me and Barnaby—my cat, I don't think I told you his name before. He's a Maine Coon mix, huge, like twenty pounds of just fluff and attitude. He sheds everywhere. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk from Costco. Costco is crazy on the weekends, though. I tried going last Saturday to get more of those rollers and some of that rotisserie chicken they have—you know, the ones that are somehow still like five bucks? But the parking lot was an absolute nightmare. People just driving the wrong way down the aisles, fighting over spots. I saw this lady in a minivan literally lay on her horn for a solid minute because someone was taking too long to load their trunk. It's just not worth the stress, honestly. I think I'm going to start going on Tuesday mornings when they open. \n\nBut yeah, at home... if I forget something, there's no one around to notice. So I couldn't really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Interrupting? No, I don't think so. If anything, I just sit there and let people talk. Some of the tenants in the buildings I work at, they will just talk your ear off if you let them. Like this one lady, Mrs. Gable, over at the Oakwood complex. I was just there to fix a leaky P-trap under her bathroom sink—which, by the way, was leaking because she kept using that harsh chemical drain cleaner. You really shouldn't use that stuff, it just eats right through the pipes if it sits there too long. \n\nAnyway, she stood in the doorway the entire time I was on the floor working, just going on and on about her niece's wedding in Ohio. Apparently, the caterer forgot the vegetarian meals, and it was this huge drama because the groom's side was mostly vegan. I've never even been to Ohio. My sister went to Cleveland once for a dental hygienist conference, but her flight got delayed like six hours on the tarmac. Airlines are just a complete mess right now. They charge you for every little thing, even just bringing a regular carry-on bag. And the seats keep getting smaller! I don't know how anyone over six feet tall even fits in them anymore. It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, no, I don't really blurt things out or interrupt. I just kind of quietly let them finish their stories.\n\nDoctor: Were these attention problems also happening at home when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I remember being this way way back in grade school. This was long before I had any job pressure or anything like that. My teachers always told my parents I was bright, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. I'd miss parts of directions, and they always had to remind me to just finish my classwork. \n\nIt reminds me of my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive collection of those little ceramic frogs. They were everywhere—on her desk, on the windowsills, even on top of the chalkboard. I always wondered where she found so many of them. Did people just know she liked frogs and buy them for her, or did she actively hunt for them at flea markets? I went to a flea market a couple of months ago looking for some vintage tools. You can sometimes find really good quality, old-school wrenches that are made of solid steel, not that cheap alloy stuff they sell now that strips the second you put some torque on it. I found a great pipe wrench, but the guy wanted way too much for it. He was trying to tell me it was an antique, but I could clearly see the brand stamp on it, and they didn't even start making that model until the eighties. People will try to pull a fast one on you if they think you don't know your stuff, especially being a woman in this line of work. You really have to stand your ground. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I've kind of always been like this, even as a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unusually restless or fidgety when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? Fidgety? No, I wouldn't say so. I'm pretty good at just sitting there, honestly. Like when I'm waiting for the parts supplier to pull an order at the warehouse. Sometimes they take forever. They have this one guy working the counter, Rick, I think his name is. He moves at the speed of molasses in January. And he always has this toothpick in his mouth, just kind of rolling it around while he's typing on the computer with one finger. I don't know how they still use those ancient computers with the green text on the black screens. It looks like something out of that movie, The Matrix. I tried to watch that movie once, but I fell asleep halfway through. Keanu Reeves is in it, right? He was in that bus movie too, Speed. Now that was a good movie. Sandra Bullock driving that bus through Los Angeles... traffic there must be a nightmare. I've heard you can sit on the freeway for three hours just to go ten miles. No thank you. I'll stick to my twenty-minute commute, even if I do get stuck behind a school bus every now and then. \n\nBut yeah, sitting still isn't really an issue for me. I just kind of wait it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, tools, or paperwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's actually becoming a real problem at work. I'm always hunting for my work tablet, or the master keys, my flashlight, or even just the one specific wrench I literally had in my hand a minute ago. It's gotten bad enough that it actually holds up jobs, and I know it makes me look careless to the property managers. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was at the Willow Creek townhomes—they have those awful beige vinyl sidings that always look dirty, no matter how much you power wash them. I don't know who picked that color, but it was a terrible choice. They should have gone with a nice slate gray or maybe a brick facade. Brick holds up so much better in this climate anyway. My uncle had a brick house in Michigan, and that thing survived some massive blizzards. Michigan winters are brutal. I remember visiting for Christmas one year and the snow was piled up higher than the mailbox. We had to shovel for two hours just to get the car out of the driveway. I don't miss the snow at all, honestly. \n\nBut yeah, I was at Willow Creek to fix a jammed garbage disposal, and I set my flashlight down... somewhere. I spent twenty minutes looking for it, tearing apart my tool bag, checking the truck. I finally found it inside the refrigerator. I must have opened the fridge to grab my water bottle and just left the flashlight on the shelf next to the mayonnaise. It's so frustrating.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Careless mistakes? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might lose my tools constantly, but when I actually have everything I need and I'm doing the repair, I'm pretty thorough. I don't really miss details in the work itself. 

Like, if I'm replacing a P-trap under a sink, I always make sure everything is lined up perfectly so it doesn't leak. Plumbing is one of those things where you really can't cut corners. You have to use the right plumber's tape, too. A lot of people just grab the standard white Teflon tape for everything, but you're supposed to use the yellow kind for gas lines. Gas lines actually terrify me, to be honest. You hear those crazy stories on the news about a house just exploding out of nowhere because of a tiny leak. There was one a few towns over a couple of years ago that completely leveled the place. The crazy part is the family wasn't even home, they were on vacation down in Florida. 

I could never live in Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando once, and the second you step out of the airport, it's like breathing through a hot, wet towel. Plus, they have those giant palmetto bugs, which is really just a fancy name for a massive flying cockroach. I don't know how people deal with that. I'd rather deal with the snow in Michigan, honestly, even if the shoveling is awful. 

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, mistakes. No, my actual repair work is pretty solid. I don't really have a problem with missing details on the job.

I spent twenty minutes looking for it, tearing apart my tool bag, checking the truck. I finally found it inside the refrigerator. I must have opened the fridge to grab my water bottle and just left the flashlight on the shelf next to the mayonnaise. It's so frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing work or other tasks because you miss details?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might lose my tools constantly, but when I actually have everything I need and I'm doing the repair, I'm pretty thorough. I don't really miss details in the work itself. \n\nLike, if I'm replacing a P-trap under a sink, I always make sure everything is lined up perfectly so it doesn't leak. Plumbing is one of those things where you really can't cut corners. You have to use the right plumber's tape, too. A lot of people just grab the standard white Teflon tape for everything, but you're supposed to use the yellow kind for gas lines. Gas lines actually terrify me, to be honest. You hear those crazy stories on the news about a house just exploding out of nowhere because of a tiny leak. There was one a few towns over a couple of years ago that completely leveled the place. The crazy part is the family wasn't even home, they were on vacation down in Florida. \n\nI could never live in Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando once, and the second you step out of the airport, it's like breathing through a hot, wet towel. Plus, they have those giant palmetto bugs, which is really just a fancy name for a massive flying cockroach. I don't know how people deal with that. I'd rather deal with the snow in Michigan, honestly, even if the shoveling is awful. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, mistakes. No, my actual repair work is pretty solid. I don't really have a problem with missing details on the job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's gotten bad enough that it actually holds up jobs, and I know it makes me look careless to the property managers. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was at the Willow Creek townhomes—they have those awful beige vinyl sidings that always look dirty, no matter how much you power wash them. I don't know who picked that color, but it was a terrible choice. They should have gone with a nice slate gray or maybe a brick facade. Brick holds up so much better in this climate anyway. My uncle had a brick house in Michigan, and that thing survived some massive blizzards. Michigan winters are brutal. I remember visiting for Christmas one year and the snow was piled up higher than the mailbox. We had to shovel for two hours just to get the car out of the driveway. I don't miss the snow at all, honestly. \n\nBut yeah, I was at Willow Creek to fix a jammed garbage disposal, and I set my flashlight down... somewhere. I spent twenty minutes looking for it, tearing apart my tool bag, checking the truck. I finally found it inside the refrigerator. I must have opened the fridge to grab my water bottle and just left the flashlight on the shelf next to the mayonnaise. It's so frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing work or other tasks because you miss details?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might lose my tools constantly, but when I actually have everything I need and I'm doing the repair, I'm pretty thorough. I don't really miss details in the work itself. \n\nLike, if I'm replacing a P-trap under a sink, I always make sure everything is lined up perfectly so it doesn't leak. Plumbing is one of those things where you really can't cut corners. You have to use the right plumber's tape, too. A lot of people just grab the standard white Teflon tape for everything, but you're supposed to use the yellow kind for gas lines. Gas lines actually terrify me, to be honest. You hear those crazy stories on the news about a house just exploding out of nowhere because of a tiny leak. There was one a few towns over a couple of years ago that completely leveled the place. The crazy part is the family wasn't even home, they were on vacation down in Florida. \n\nI could never live in Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando once, and the second you step out of the airport, it's like breathing through a hot, wet towel. Plus, they have those giant palmetto bugs, which is really just a fancy name for a massive flying cockroach. I don't know how people deal with that. I'd rather deal with the snow in Michigan, honestly, even if the shoveling is awful. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, mistakes. No, my actual repair work is pretty solid. I don't really have a problem with missing details on the job.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs a child, did these attention problems cause you to struggle in school, like getting poor grades or needing extra help?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / maintenance_or_repair_worker_general / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"I remember being this way back in grade school, long before any of this job pressure—teachers said I was bright but always misplaced papers, missed parts of directions, and needed reminders to finish classwork.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It's not just the job pressure now, it's always been like this. I remember being exactly the same way back in grade school. My teachers would always tell my parents at conferences that I was bright, but I was constantly misplacing my assignments or I'd miss half the directions for a project. They always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork instead of just staring out the window. 

I used to love looking out the window in Mrs. Gable's class in fourth grade. We had this big oak tree right outside, and I'd watch the squirrels chasing each other around the trunk. Squirrels are actually pretty fascinating if you watch them long enough. They have this whole system for burying acorns, but apparently, they forget where they put half of them, which is how new trees grow. Kind of funny, right? A whole forest just from forgetting where you put your lunch. I guess I have a lot in common with squirrels. 

But I used to feed the squirrels at the park near my old apartment. I'd bring unsalted peanuts—you have to get the unsalted ones, the salt is bad for them. I'd sit on this green bench, and there was this one squirrel with a little notch in his ear who would come right up to me and take it out of my hand. My neighbor told me I was going to get rabies doing that. Rabies is a terrible way to go, honestly. I saw a documentary about it once, and once you show symptoms, it's basically a wrap. You get terrified of water and everything. It's wild how a tiny bite from an animal can do that to a human brain. 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, school. Yeah, poor grades because I'd lose my papers or not finish things. Always needed extra help staying on track.

Like I said, I was mostly on my own, just watching TV or whatever until she got back.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have these attention or forgetfulness problems in places other than work, like at home or in social situations, now as an adult?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't really know. Like I said before, I haven't really noticed it at home. It's just me and Barnaby, so... if I forget to do the dishes or leave a cabinet open, there's nobody yelling at me about it. \n\nBut social situations... I guess? I don't really go out much anymore. My friend Sarah and I used to go to this trivia night at the pub downtown, but they changed the host a few months ago. The old guy, Mike, he was great. He'd always throw in a category about 80s movies, which is totally my jam. I can quote almost the entire script of *The Princess Bride*. But the new host, he's just... he talks way too fast, and the microphone is always echoing, and he asks all these obscure sports questions. I don't care about who won the World Series in 1994, you know? The strike year, right? Or was that '95? Anyway, Sarah stopped wanting to go because the food went downhill, too. They changed the cheese they use on the nachos. It used to be real cheddar, but now it's that fake liquid stuff that comes out of a pump. I can't stand that stuff. It tastes like plastic. \n\nSo yeah, I don't really know about social stuff. I just kind of keep to myself mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you have to move around a lot, even when you’re trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. I mean, my job keeps me moving all day anyway. I'm always fixing something, climbing a ladder, crawling under a sink. You wouldn't believe the things people flush down toilets in office buildings. Last week, I had to snake this drain on the fourth floor, and I pulled out an entire pair of sunglasses. Like, the big chunky plastic ones. Who drops their sunglasses in the toilet and just flushes? It took me an hour to get the auger unstuck. \n\nThe maintenance closet on that floor is a disaster, too. The guy who had this route before me, Dave, he never organized anything. Just threw all the spare parts into this one giant bin. I spent a whole afternoon just sorting the PVC fittings from the copper ones. Copper is so expensive right now, you know? You have to keep track of it or people will literally steal the pipes right out of the walls if a building is empty. My uncle had that happen to a rental property he owned out in Arizona. Just gutted the place overnight. He ended up selling it at a loss and moving to Florida. Florida seems too humid for me, though. I don't like being sweaty all the time. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm pretty tired by the time I get home. I just sit on the couch. I don't really feel like I need to move around or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these attention or forgetfulness problems in your daily life outside of work, such as at home?\n\nPatient: I think you asked me this a minute ago, right? Or something like it. Like I said, I don't really know if I have those problems at home. It's just me and Barnaby—my cat, I don't think I told you his name before. He's a Maine Coon mix, huge, like twenty pounds of just fluff and attitude. He sheds everywhere. I have to buy those sticky lint rollers in bulk from Costco. Costco is crazy on the weekends, though. I tried going last Saturday to get more of those rollers and some of that rotisserie chicken they have—you know, the ones that are somehow still like five bucks? But the parking lot was an absolute nightmare. People just driving the wrong way down the aisles, fighting over spots. I saw this lady in a minivan literally lay on her horn for a solid minute because someone was taking too long to load their trunk. It's just not worth the stress, honestly. I think I'm going to start going on Tuesday mornings when they open. \n\nBut yeah, at home... if I forget something, there's no one around to notice. So I couldn't really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt people or blurt out answers before they finish asking the question?\n\nPatient: Interrupting? No, I don't think so. If anything, I just sit there and let people talk. Some of the tenants in the buildings I work at, they will just talk your ear off if you let them. Like this one lady, Mrs. Gable, over at the Oakwood complex. I was just there to fix a leaky P-trap under her bathroom sink—which, by the way, was leaking because she kept using that harsh chemical drain cleaner. You really shouldn't use that stuff, it just eats right through the pipes if it sits there too long. \n\nAnyway, she stood in the doorway the entire time I was on the floor working, just going on and on about her niece's wedding in Ohio. Apparently, the caterer forgot the vegetarian meals, and it was this huge drama because the groom's side was mostly vegan. I've never even been to Ohio. My sister went to Cleveland once for a dental hygienist conference, but her flight got delayed like six hours on the tarmac. Airlines are just a complete mess right now. They charge you for every little thing, even just bringing a regular carry-on bag. And the seats keep getting smaller! I don't know how anyone over six feet tall even fits in them anymore. It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, no, I don't really blurt things out or interrupt. I just kind of quietly let them finish their stories.\n\nDoctor: Were these attention problems also happening at home when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I remember being this way way back in grade school. This was long before I had any job pressure or anything like that. My teachers always told my parents I was bright, but I was constantly misplacing my papers. I'd miss parts of directions, and they always had to remind me to just finish my classwork. \n\nIt reminds me of my third-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins. She had this massive collection of those little ceramic frogs. They were everywhere—on her desk, on the windowsills, even on top of the chalkboard. I always wondered where she found so many of them. Did people just know she liked frogs and buy them for her, or did she actively hunt for them at flea markets? I went to a flea market a couple of months ago looking for some vintage tools. You can sometimes find really good quality, old-school wrenches that are made of solid steel, not that cheap alloy stuff they sell now that strips the second you put some torque on it. I found a great pipe wrench, but the guy wanted way too much for it. He was trying to tell me it was an antique, but I could clearly see the brand stamp on it, and they didn't even start making that model until the eighties. People will try to pull a fast one on you if they think you don't know your stuff, especially being a woman in this line of work. You really have to stand your ground. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I've kind of always been like this, even as a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel unusually restless or fidgety when you have to sit still?\n\nPatient: Restless? Fidgety? No, I wouldn't say so. I'm pretty good at just sitting there, honestly. Like when I'm waiting for the parts supplier to pull an order at the warehouse. Sometimes they take forever. They have this one guy working the counter, Rick, I think his name is. He moves at the speed of molasses in January. And he always has this toothpick in his mouth, just kind of rolling it around while he's typing on the computer with one finger. I don't know how they still use those ancient computers with the green text on the black screens. It looks like something out of that movie, The Matrix. I tried to watch that movie once, but I fell asleep halfway through. Keanu Reeves is in it, right? He was in that bus movie too, Speed. Now that was a good movie. Sandra Bullock driving that bus through Los Angeles... traffic there must be a nightmare. I've heard you can sit on the freeway for three hours just to go ten miles. No thank you. I'll stick to my twenty-minute commute, even if I do get stuck behind a school bus every now and then. \n\nBut yeah, sitting still isn't really an issue for me. I just kind of wait it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for tasks, like keys, tools, or paperwork?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's actually becoming a real problem at work. I'm always hunting for my work tablet, or the master keys, my flashlight, or even just the one specific wrench I literally had in my hand a minute ago. It's gotten bad enough that it actually holds up jobs, and I know it makes me look careless to the property managers. \n\nLike last Tuesday, I was at the Willow Creek townhomes—they have those awful beige vinyl sidings that always look dirty, no matter how much you power wash them. I don't know who picked that color, but it was a terrible choice. They should have gone with a nice slate gray or maybe a brick facade. Brick holds up so much better in this climate anyway. My uncle had a brick house in Michigan, and that thing survived some massive blizzards. Michigan winters are brutal. I remember visiting for Christmas one year and the snow was piled up higher than the mailbox. We had to shovel for two hours just to get the car out of the driveway. I don't miss the snow at all, honestly. \n\nBut yeah, I was at Willow Creek to fix a jammed garbage disposal, and I set my flashlight down... somewhere. I spent twenty minutes looking for it, tearing apart my tool bag, checking the truck. I finally found it inside the refrigerator. I must have opened the fridge to grab my water bottle and just left the flashlight on the shelf next to the mayonnaise. It's so frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes when doing work or other tasks because you miss details?\n\nPatient: Careless mistakes? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I might lose my tools constantly, but when I actually have everything I need and I'm doing the repair, I'm pretty thorough. I don't really miss details in the work itself. \n\nLike, if I'm replacing a P-trap under a sink, I always make sure everything is lined up perfectly so it doesn't leak. Plumbing is one of those things where you really can't cut corners. You have to use the right plumber's tape, too. A lot of people just grab the standard white Teflon tape for everything, but you're supposed to use the yellow kind for gas lines. Gas lines actually terrify me, to be honest. You hear those crazy stories on the news about a house just exploding out of nowhere because of a tiny leak. There was one a few towns over a couple of years ago that completely leveled the place. The crazy part is the family wasn't even home, they were on vacation down in Florida. \n\nI could never live in Florida, though, the humidity is just unbearable. I went to Orlando once, and the second you step out of the airport, it's like breathing through a hot, wet towel. Plus, they have those giant palmetto bugs, which is really just a fancy name for a massive flying cockroach. I don't know how people deal with that. I'd rather deal with the snow in Michigan, honestly, even if the shoveling is awful. \n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, mistakes. No, my actual repair work is pretty solid. I don't really have a problem with missing details on the job.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did these attention problems cause you to struggle in school, like getting poor grades or needing extra help?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's not just the job pressure now, it's always been like this. I remember being exactly the same way back in grade school. My teachers would always tell my parents at conferences that I was bright, but I was constantly misplacing my assignments or I'd miss half the directions for a project. They always had to remind me to actually finish my classwork instead of just staring out the window. \n\nI used to love looking out the window in Mrs. Gable's class in fourth grade. We had this big oak tree right outside, and I'd watch the squirrels chasing each other around the trunk. Squirrels are actually pretty fascinating if you watch them long enough. They have this whole system for burying acorns, but apparently, they forget where they put half of them, which is how new trees grow. Kind of funny, right? A whole forest just from forgetting where you put your lunch. I guess I have a lot in common with squirrels. \n\nBut I used to feed the squirrels at the park near my old apartment. I'd bring unsalted peanuts—you have to get the unsalted ones, the salt is bad for them. I'd sit on this green bench, and there was this one squirrel with a little notch in his ear who would come right up to me and take it out of my hand. My neighbor told me I was going to get rabies doing that. Rabies is a terrible way to go, honestly. I saw a documentary about it once, and once you show symptoms, it's basically a wrap. You get terrified of water and everything. It's wild how a tiny bite from an animal can do that to a human brain. \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, school. Yeah, poor grades because I'd lose my papers or not finish things. Always needed extra help staying on track.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Fidget or move around? No, not really. I mean, I'm on my feet all day for work anyway, so I don't really have that problem of needing to move when I'm supposed to sit still. When I do get a chance to sit, I'm usually just exhausted. 

Like when I get home, I just collapse on the couch. I bought this huge sectional last year, it's that microfiber material that's supposed to be easy to clean. Which is good, because my cat, Barnaby, sheds like crazy. He's an orange tabby, and they always say orange cats share one brain cell, which is honestly so true. Yesterday he spent twenty minutes fighting his own reflection in the oven door. I don't know how he doesn't realize it's him. 

Animals are so funny like that. My cousin had a parrot that would mimic the sound of the microwave beeping, and they'd always get up to check their food only to realize the bird tricked them. Parrots live forever, too. Like, you have to put them in your will because they outlive you. Imagine inheriting a bird that just screams in your dead relative's voice. That would be so creepy. I think I'll just stick to cats. They mostly just sleep anyway. 

But yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, fidgeting. No, I don't really do that. I don't have a problem sitting still.
